# Skin 7: Praxicapades — Applied Mathematics, Statistics & Modeling

## 1. Domain Overview & Core Fault Lines

Applied mathematics, statistics, and mathematical modeling bridge formal abstraction and messy empirical reality—turning noisy data, physical laws, and strategic decisions into tractable equations, inferences, and algorithms. Five profound fault lines organize the field:

1. **Breiman's "Two Cultures" — Generative Data Modeling vs. Algorithmic Prediction:** Should statistical science center on interpretable, probabilistic stochastic models of how data were generated, or prioritize out-of-sample predictive accuracy via flexible algorithmic and machine-learning black boxes? (Breiman 2001; Shmueli 2010; Efron 2020).
2. **Foundations of Inference — Frequentist Error Control vs. Bayesian Coherence vs. Likelihood/Fiducial Alternatives:** Does scientific objectivity demand long-run error-rate guarantees without subjective priors (Neyman–Pearson/Fisher/Mayo), or coherent subjective/objective belief updating via Bayes' theorem (Jeffreys/Savage/Jaynes/Gelman)?
3. **Causal Inference Paradigms — Potential Outcomes vs. Structural Causal DAGs vs. Mechanistic Differential Equations:** How do we move from correlation to intervention—through randomized experiments and Neyman–Rubin potential outcomes, Pearl's graphical do-calculus, or mechanistic dynamical ODE/PDE models? (Rubin 1974; Pearl 2000; Imbens 2020).
4. **Asymptotic Analytical Craft vs. Numerical & Non-Asymptotic Computation:** Should applied mathematicians prize perturbation theory, matched asymptotics, scaling laws, and exact convex formulations, or leverage Monte Carlo, automatic differentiation, and brute-force optimization? (Bender & Orszag 1978; Boyd & Vandenberghe 2004; Wainwright 2019).
5. **Model Parsimony & Domain Grounding vs. Overparameterized Expressiveness:** Following George Box ("all models are wrong, some are useful"), should we prefer low-dimensional, interpretable, bias-variance-balanced models—or embrace double-descent overparameterized models that generalize via implicit regularization? (Box 1976; Belkin et al. 2019).

## 2. Categories

1. **Philosophy of Modeling & Abstraction** — Box's dictum, parsimony vs. double descent, mechanistic vs. phenomenological models, asymptotic vs. exact thinking, and model humility.
2. **Statistical Inference & Uncertainty** — Frequentist vs. Bayesian, p-values and significance reform, prior philosophy, high-dimensional asymptotics, and nonparametric vs. parametric modeling.
3. **Causality, Design & Evidence** — Potential outcomes vs. DAGs, RCTs vs. observational adjustment, identification vs. structural estimation, prediction vs. explanation, and preregistration.
4. **Optimization, Inverse Problems & Control** — Convex vs. non-convex optimization, inverse-problem regularization, optimal control vs. RL, minimax robustness vs. average-case, and sparsity.
5. **Dynamical Systems, Stochastics & Uncertainty** — Deterministic chaos vs. stochastic modeling, heavy tails & extreme risk, information theory & compression, network & agent complexity, and scientific ML hybrids.
6. **Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics** — Proof rigor vs. working heuristics, reproducible data pipelines, algorithmic fairness & model governance, domain immersion, and communication of uncertainty.

## 3. Topics (34 Topics)

### Category 1: Philosophy of Modeling & Abstraction

#### `PR-01` — Breiman's Two Cultures: Generative Models vs. Algorithmic Prediction
- **Category:** Philosophy of Modeling & Abstraction
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Generative Stochastic Data Models` | `Hybrid Interpretable Predictive Craft` | `Algorithmic Black-Box Prediction First`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Should statisticians model the underlying probabilistic mechanism generating the data, or judge models primarily by cross-validated predictive accuracy regardless of internal opacity?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Generative Stochastic Data Models:* Explicit probabilistic models encode domain structure, enable counterfactual reasoning, and expose exactly how assumptions fail.
  - *Hybrid Interpretable Predictive Craft:* Pair principled probabilistic modeling with cross-validated predictive checks—using flexible ML as a diagnostic benchmark for parametric misspecification (Gelman / Efron).
  - *Algorithmic Black-Box Prediction First:* Real data-generating processes are hopelessly complex; insisting on neat parametric formulas sacrifices massive real-world predictive accuracy (Breiman).
- **Primary citations:** L. Breiman (2001), "Statistical Modeling: The Two Cultures," *Stat. Sci.*; B. Efron (2020), "Prediction, Estimation, and Attribution," *JASA*; G. Shmueli (2010), *Stat. Sci.*

#### `PR-02` — Parsimony & Occam's Razor vs. Overparameterized Double Descent
- **Category:** Philosophy of Modeling & Abstraction
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Strict Low-Dimensional Parsimony` | `Context-Dependent Capacity Control` | `Embrace Overparameterized Double Descent`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Classical statistics warns against fitting more parameters than observations ("with four parameters I can fit an elephant"), yet modern deep learning thrives in wildly overparameterized regimes—which principle guides you?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Strict Low-Dimensional Parsimony:* Lean models with physically meaningful parameters generalize cleanly out-of-distribution and resist spurious correlations.
  - *Context-Dependent Capacity Control:* Regularization and effective degrees of freedom—not raw parameter count—govern generalization; choose parsimony for small scientific data and rich capacity for massive datasets.
  - *Embrace Overparameterized Double Descent:* Past the interpolation threshold, implicit gradient-descent regularization produces smooth, remarkably generalizable models classical bias–variance curves missed.
- **Primary citations:** G. E. P. Box (1976), *JASA*; M. Belkin et al. (2019), "Reconciling Modern Machine-Learning Practice and the Classical Bias–Variance Trade-Off," *PNAS*; J. von Neumann's four-parameter elephant aphorism (Dyson 2004).

#### `PR-03` — Mechanistic Equations vs. Data-Driven Empirical Discovery
- **Category:** Philosophy of Modeling & Abstraction
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `First-Principles Conservation & ODE/PDEs` | `Grey-Box Equation Discovery (SINDy)` | `Purely Empirical Statistical Learning`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** When modeling a complex phenomenon (epidemics, finance, physiology, fluids), should you derive equations from conservation laws first or let data dictate functional form?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *First-Principles Conservation & ODE/PDEs:* Conservation of mass, energy, and momentum provides non-negotiable structure that extrapolates far beyond observed data ranges.
  - *Grey-Box Equation Discovery (SINDy):* Combine known physical scaffolds with sparse symbolic regression and residual learning to discover missing closure terms cleanly.
  - *Purely Empirical Statistical Learning:* In social, economic, and biological systems, assumed "first-principles" laws are often brittle idealizations; flexible empirical estimation beats wrong dogma.
- **Primary citations:** S. L. Brunton, J. L. Proctor & J. N. Kutz (2016), *PNAS* (SINDy); C. C. Lin & L. A. Segel (1974), *Mathematics Applied to Deterministic Problems in the Natural Sciences*.

#### `PR-04` — Asymptotics & Scaling Laws vs. Exact Numerical Precision
- **Category:** Philosophy of Modeling & Abstraction
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Back-of-the-Envelope & Matched Asymptotics` | `Asymptotics Validated Numerically` | `Exact Full-Scale Numerical Solution`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Does true applied-mathematical mastery lie in dimensional analysis, perturbation series, and scaling exponents, or in solving the full unapproximated problem numerically?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Back-of-the-Envelope & Matched Asymptotics:* Buckingham $\Pi$, boundary-layer scaling, and two-term perturbation series reveal dominant balances and universal regimes a table of numbers hides.
  - *Asymptotics Validated Numerically:* Derive analytical scaling limits to understand the regimes and test code, then compute full numerical solutions in the transitional $O(1)$ middle.
  - *Exact Full-Scale Numerical Solution:* Real engineering parameters rarely sit at $\varepsilon \to 0$; modern solvers handle the full nonlinear geometry without fragile asymptotic expansions.
- **Primary citations:** C. M. Bender & S. A. Orszag (1978), *Advanced Mathematical Methods for Scientists and Engineers*; G. I. Barenblatt (1996), *Scaling, Self-Similarity, and Intermediate Asymptotics*.

#### `PR-05` — Purpose of Modeling Compass
- **Category:** Philosophy of Modeling & Abstraction
- **Geometry:** `quad-compass`
- **Poles:** `Accurate Out-of-Sample Forecast` | `Mechanistic Insight & Explanation` | `Optimal Decision & Control` | `Stress-Testing & Scenario Exploration`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Following Joshua Epstein's "Why Model?", which modeling purpose anchors your evaluation criteria?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Accurate Out-of-Sample Forecast:* A model proves its mettle by sharp, calibrated predictions on unseen future observations.
  - *Mechanistic Insight & Explanation:* Even a crude model succeeds brilliantly if it isolates the causal feedback loop driving an otherwise baffling phenomenon.
  - *Optimal Decision & Control:* Models exist to guide real choices under loss functions—what matters is expected utility and regret, not abstract fit.
  - *Stress-Testing & Scenario Exploration:* In deeply uncertain systems, models serve as disciplined "what-if" laboratories exposing tail vulnerabilities and unintended consequences.
- **Primary citations:** J. M. Epstein (2008), "Why Model?," *JASSS*; G. E. P. Box & N. R. Draper (1987), *Empirical Model-Building and Response Surfaces*.

---

### Category 2: Statistical Inference & Uncertainty

#### `PR-06` — Foundations of Statistical Inference
- **Category:** Statistical Inference & Uncertainty
- **Geometry:** `triangle`
- **Poles:** `Frequentist Error-Rate Control` | `Bayesian Coherent Updating` | `Pure Likelihood & Pragmatic Pluralism`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** What is the soundest mathematical foundation for drawing inductive conclusions from finite, noisy data?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Frequentist Error-Rate Control:* Scientific claims require objective, repeatable severity and long-run Type I/II error guarantees without injecting arbitrary prior beliefs (Neyman–Pearson, Mayo).
  - *Bayesian Coherent Updating:* Cox's and Savage's theorems show that probability updating via Bayes' rule is the unique coherent calculus of uncertainty, directly quantifying parameter credibility.
  - *Pure Likelihood & Pragmatic Pluralism:* Likelihood ratios carry the direct evidential force of the data; wise statisticians calibrate Bayesian models with frequentist checks and vice versa (Fisher, Box, Gelman).
- **Primary citations:** J. Neyman & E. S. Pearson (1933), *Phil. Trans. R. Soc. A*; L. J. Savage (1954), *The Foundations of Statistics*; D. G. Mayo (2018), *Statistical Inference as Severe Testing*; A. Gelman & C. R. Shalizi (2013), *Br. J. Math. Stat. Psychol.*

#### `PR-07` — Reform of $p$-Values & Null-Hypothesis Significance Testing
- **Category:** Statistical Inference & Uncertainty
- **Geometry:** `quad-compass`
- **Poles:** `Retain p < 0.05 with Discipline` | `Tighten Threshold (p < 0.005)` | `Replace with Effect Sizes & Intervals` | `Switch to Bayes Factors & Posteriors`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Amid the replication crisis and widespread $p$-hacking, how should applied statistics reform null-hypothesis significance testing?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Retain p < 0.05 with Discipline:* Properly preregistered $p$-values with multiple-testing corrections aren't broken—bad research incentives and undisclosed flexibility are.
  - *Tighten Threshold (p < 0.005):* Raising the default discovery bar from $0.05$ to $0.005$ immediately slashes false-positive rates across empirical sciences (Benjamin et al.).
  - *Replace with Effect Sizes & Intervals:* Dichotomous "significant vs. non-significant" cliffs distort reality; report continuous compatibility intervals, effect magnitudes, and practical importance (ASA 2016; Amrhein et al.).
  - *Switch to Bayes Factors & Posteriors:* Only Bayes factors and hierarchical posterior distributions weigh competing hypotheses directly and incorporate base-rate plausibility cleanly.
- **Primary citations:** R. L. Wasserstein & N. A. Lazar (2016), "The ASA Statement on $p$-Values," *Am. Stat.*; D. J. Benjamin et al. (2018), *Nat. Hum. Behav.*; V. Amrhein, S. Greenland & B. McShane (2019), *Nature*.

#### `PR-08` — Bayesian Prior Philosophy
- **Category:** Statistical Inference & Uncertainty
- **Geometry:** `triangle`
- **Poles:** `Objective / Reference Non-Informative Priors` | `Weakly Informative Regularizing Priors` | `Elicited Subjective Domain Priors`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** When specifying a Bayesian model, how should priors be constructed?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Objective / Reference Non-Informative Priors:* Maximum-entropy, Jeffreys, and reference priors let the data speak with minimal analyst subjectivity while preserving invariance (Jaynes, Berger).
  - *Weakly Informative Regularizing Priors:* Flat priors are actually absurdly dogmatic on extreme scales; weakly informative priors rule out physically impossible values and stabilize computation (Gelman).
  - *Elicited Subjective Domain Priors:* Pretending ignorance throws away hard-won scientific knowledge; honestly elicit and document domain priors and run sensitivity audits.
- **Primary citations:** E. T. Jaynes (2003), *Probability Theory: The Logic of Science*; J. O. Berger (2006), *Bayesian Anal.*; A. Gelman, D. Simpson & M. Betancourt (2017), *Entropy*.

#### `PR-09` — High-Dimensional Regime ($p \gg n$): Non-Asymptotic vs. Classical Asymptotics
- **Category:** Statistical Inference & Uncertainty
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Classical Fixed-p Large-n Asymptotics` | `Proportional High-Dim Limits (p/n → γ)` | `Strict Finite-Sample Non-Asymptotic Bounds`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** When feature dimensions rival or exceed sample sizes, which theoretical lens gives the most trustworthy statistical guarantees?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Classical Fixed-p Large-n Asymptotics:* Classic central-limit and Wilks theorems remain clean, sharp, and accurate wherever sample sizes genuinely dominate parameter count.
  - *Proportional High-Dim Limits (p/n → γ):* Random matrix theory and proportional asymptotics capture exact modern phenomena (like Marchenko–Pastur spectra and high-dimensional bias) invisible to classical limits.
  - *Strict Finite-Sample Non-Asymptotic Bounds:* Concentration inequalities and finite-sample minimax bounds hold at the exact $n$ and $p$ you actually have—never relying on an infinite limit.
- **Primary citations:** M. J. Wainwright (2019), *High-Dimensional Statistics: A Non-Asymptotic Viewpoint*; D. L. Donoho & A. Montanari (2016), *Probab. Theory Relat. Fields*.

#### `PR-10` — Parametric Structure vs. Nonparametric & Distribution-Free Guarantees
- **Category:** Statistical Inference & Uncertainty
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Structured Parametric Models` | `Semiparametric Targeted Efficiency` | `Distribution-Free & Conformal Guarantees`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Should we lean on crisp parametric families for statistical efficiency, or demand distribution-free guarantees (bootstrap, permutation tests, conformal prediction) that survive misspecification?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Structured Parametric Models:* Well-chosen parametric and hierarchical models extract maximum signal from scarce data and support clean scientific interpretation.
  - *Semiparametric Targeted Efficiency:* Target low-dimensional scientific estimands with double-robust/debiased estimators while estimating nuisance functions flexibly (van der Laan, Chernozhukov).
  - *Distribution-Free & Conformal Guarantees:* Gaussian and linear assumptions fail routinely in the wild; bootstrap, rank tests, and conformal prediction guarantee finite-sample coverage under minimal exchangeability.
- **Primary citations:** B. Efron (1979), *Ann. Stat.* (Bootstrap); V. Vovk, A. Gammerman & G. Shafer (2005), *Algorithmic Learning in a Random World* (Conformal Prediction); V. Chernozhukov et al. (2018), *Econom. J.* (DML).

---

### Category 3: Causality, Design & Evidence

#### `PR-11` — Causal Inference Framework Allegiance
- **Category:** Causality, Design & Evidence
- **Geometry:** `triangle`
- **Poles:** `Potential Outcomes (Neyman–Rubin)` | `Structural Causal Graphs (Pearl DAGs)` | `Dynamical & Structural Econometric Systems`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** What mathematical language best formalizes cause and effect?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Potential Outcomes (Neyman–Rubin):* Anchoring causal estimands directly to well-defined treatment interventions and experimental designs keeps causal claims concrete and testable (Imbens & Rubin).
  - *Structural Causal Graphs (Pearl DAGs):* Graphical $d$-separation and do-calculus lay bare confounding, colliders, and mediation transparently—exposing hidden assumptions prose disguises (Pearl).
  - *Dynamical & Structural Econometric Systems:* Complex feedback loops, equilibria, and continuous-time dynamics require explicit structural equations or dynamical systems rather than static acyclic graphs.
- **Primary citations:** D. B. Rubin (1974), *J. Educ. Psychol.*; J. Pearl (2000/2009), *Causality*; G. W. Imbens (2020), "Potential Outcome and Directed Acyclic Graph Approaches to Causality," *J. Econ. Lit.*

#### `PR-12` — Randomized Experiments vs. Observational Causal Identification
- **Category:** Causality, Design & Evidence
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `No Causation Without Manipulation (RCT Gold Standard)` | `Quasi-Experimental Design-Based Pluralism` | `Observational Adjustment & Sensitivity Analysis`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** How far should we trust causal claims drawn from observational data when randomized controlled trials are unethical, infeasible, or too narrow in external validity?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *No Causation Without Manipulation (RCT Gold Standard):* Unmeasured confounding haunts every observational regression; randomization is the only clean foundation for causal inference (Holland; Rubin).
  - *Quasi-Experimental Design-Based Pluralism:* Natural experiments—regression discontinuity, instrumental variables, difference-in-differences—deliver credible causal identification in real-world settings (Angrist & Pischke).
  - *Observational Adjustment & Sensitivity Analysis:* Many vital questions (climate, smoking, macro-policy) forbid RCTs; rigorous DAG adjustment paired with formal sensitivity bounds for hidden bias yields actionable truth (Rosenbaum, Hernán).
- **Primary citations:** P. W. Holland (1986), *JASA*; J. D. Angrist & J.-S. Pischke (2010), "The Credibility Revolution in Empirical Economics," *J. Econ. Perspect.*; M. A. Hernán & J. M. Robins (2020), *Causal Inference: What If*.

#### `PR-13` — Reduced-Form Identification vs. Structural Modeling
- **Category:** Causality, Design & Evidence
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Design-Based Reduced-Form Clarity` | `Sufficient-Statistics Bridge` | `Full Structural Counterfactual Models`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Should applied researchers prioritize rock-solid local causal identification with minimal assumptions, or build full structural models capable of evaluating entirely new out-of-sample policies?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Design-Based Reduced-Form Clarity:* Clean natural experiments answer one sharp question credibly without smuggling fragile behavioral or equilibrium assumptions into the results.
  - *Sufficient-Statistics Bridge:* Combine transparent reduced-form elasticities with minimal theoretical formulas to evaluate welfare and counterfactuals cleanly (Chetty).
  - *Full Structural Counterfactual Models:* Local average treatment effects don't tell you what happens under a brand-new policy or equilibrium shift; structural models are indispensable for deep policy design (Heckman).
- **Primary citations:** J. J. Heckman (2010), *J. Econ. Lit.*; R. Chetty (2009), *Annu. Rev. Econ.*; J. D. Angrist & J.-S. Pischke (2010).

#### `PR-14` — Explanation vs. Prediction Priority
- **Category:** Causality, Design & Evidence
- **Geometry:** `linear-sliders`
- **Poles:** `Causal & Mechanistic Explanation` | `Calibrated Predictive Accuracy`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Because the best predictive model often differs from the true causal model (e.g., due to regularization bias or collider features), how strongly do you prioritize each goal independently?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Causal & Mechanistic Explanation:* Science aims to know what levers move the system—without causal understanding, predictions shatter the moment the environment shifts.
  - *Calibrated Predictive Accuracy:* Sharp, rigorously scored out-of-sample forecasts keep theories honest and immediately power decisions in medicine, weather, and engineering.
- **Primary citations:** G. Shmueli (2010), "To Explain or to Predict?," *Stat. Sci.*; J. Hofman et al. (2021), *Nature*.

#### `PR-15` — Preregistration & Multiverse Analysis vs. Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA)
- **Category:** Causality, Design & Evidence
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Tukeyan Open Exploratory Craft` | `Strict Split: Exploratory vs. Confirmatory` | `Mandatory Preregistration & Multiverse Audits`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** John Tukey celebrated exploratory data detective work, while modern reproducibility reformers warn against the "garden of forking paths"—how should analysts balance discovery and discipline?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Tukeyan Open Exploratory Craft:* Rigidly locking analysis plans before inspecting data blinds analysts to outliers, measurement bugs, and unexpected gold lurking in plots (Tukey).
  - *Strict Split: Exploratory vs. Confirmatory:* Explore freely on training/pilot folds, label hypotheses transparently, and reserve clean held-out data or fresh samples for confirmatory testing.
  - *Mandatory Preregistration & Multiverse Audits:* Researcher degrees of freedom silently inflate false discoveries; preregistered protocols and full specification-curve/multiverse checks are essential for trustworthy evidence.
- **Primary citations:** J. W. Tukey (1977), *Exploratory Data Analysis*; A. Gelman & E. Loken (2014), "The Statistical Crisis in Science," *Am. Sci.*; S. Steegen et al. (2016), *Perspect. Psychol. Sci.* (Multiverse Analysis).

---

### Category 4: Optimization, Inverse Problems & Control

#### `PR-16` — Convex Guarantees vs. Non-Convex Empirical Power
- **Category:** Optimization, Inverse Problems & Control
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Formulate Convex Problems Only` | `Convex Relaxations + Local Refinement` | `Embrace Non-Convex Landscape & SGD`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Should applied mathematicians work hard to cast problems as convex programs with global optimality certificates, or embrace non-convex formulations solved pragmatically by stochastic first-order methods?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Formulate Convex Problems Only:* Once a problem is convex, global optimality, duality certificates, and polynomial-time reliability follow automatically—reformulate until it's convex (Boyd).
  - *Convex Relaxations + Local Refinement:* Use semidefinite or $\ell_1$ relaxations to obtain provable bounds and smart initializations, then polish with fast non-convex solvers.
  - *Embrace Non-Convex Landscape & SGD:* Many modern non-convex problems (matrix completion, deep nets, tensor factorization) have benign landscapes where simple SGD finds globally competitive solutions at massive scale.
- **Primary citations:** S. Boyd & L. Vandenberghe (2004), *Convex Optimization*; R. T. Rockafellar (1993), *SIAM Rev.*; C. Jin et al. (2017) & modern non-convex optimization literature.

#### `PR-17` — Solving Ill-Posed Inverse Problems
- **Category:** Optimization, Inverse Problems & Control
- **Geometry:** `triangle`
- **Poles:** `Classical Variational Regularization (Tikhonov/TV)` | `Full Bayesian Inverse Posterior` | `Learned Plug-and-Play & Diffusion Priors`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** When reconstructing hidden interiors from noisy indirect measurements (medical imaging, seismology, tomography), how should you tame ill-posedness?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Classical Variational Regularization (Tikhonov/TV):* Explicit Sobolev or total-variation penalties offer transparent stability guarantees, fast solvers, and zero risk of hallucinating fake features.
  - *Full Bayesian Inverse Posterior:* An inverse problem rarely has one answer—only sampling the full posterior distribution honestly reveals multimodal uncertainty and null-space ambiguities (Stuart).
  - *Learned Plug-and-Play & Diffusion Priors:* Data-driven generative priors capture complex natural-image and anatomical structure vastly better than handcrafted smoothness penalties.
- **Primary citations:** A. N. Tikhonov & V. Y. Arsenin (1977), *Solutions of Ill-Posed Problems*; A. M. Stuart (2010), "Inverse Problems: A Bayesian Perspective," *Acta Numer.*; S. Arridge et al. (2019), *Acta Numer.*

#### `PR-18` — Classical Optimal Control & MPC vs. Reinforcement Learning
- **Category:** Optimization, Inverse Problems & Control
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Model-Based Optimal Control & Lyapunov Safety` | `Learning-Augmented MPC` | `Model-Free Deep Reinforcement Learning`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** When steering physical, robotic, or economic dynamical systems under uncertainty, do you trust classical control theory or learned RL policies?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Model-Based Optimal Control & Lyapunov Safety:* LQR, Kalman filters, $H_\infty$, and Model Predictive Control provide provable stability, constraint satisfaction, and sample efficiency impossible for black-box RL.
  - *Learning-Augmented MPC:* Use data-driven dynamics learning or value approximation inside a certified MPC/control-barrier-function safety envelope.
  - *Model-Free Deep Reinforcement Learning:* When contact dynamics, perception, or multi-agent interactions defy neat analytical models, end-to-end RL discovers superhuman control policies automatically.
- **Primary citations:** R. Bellman (1957), *Dynamic Programming*; R. E. Kalman (1960); B. Recht (2019), "A Tour of Reinforcement Learning: The View from Continuous Control," *Annu. Rev. Control Robot. Auton. Syst.*

#### `PR-19` — Worst-Case Minimax Robustness vs. Average-Case Expected Utility
- **Category:** Optimization, Inverse Problems & Control
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Optimize Average Expected Performance` | `Distributionally Robust Optimization (DRO)` | `Strict Minimax Worst-Case Guarantees`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Under model misspecification and shifting environments, should decisions maximize expected payoff under a nominal distribution or protect against adversarial worst cases?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Optimize Average Expected Performance:* Optimizing for paranoid worst-case scenarios wastes huge performance on routine everyday operation.
  - *Distributionally Robust Optimization (DRO):* Hedge against plausible Wasserstein or KL-ball shifts around the empirical distribution—balancing conservatism with efficiency cleanly.
  - *Strict Minimax Worst-Case Guarantees:* In safety-critical control, finance, and infrastructure, surviving adversarial tail regimes matters far more than shaving 2% off average cost (Wald; Ben-Tal et al.).
- **Primary citations:** A. Wald (1950), *Statistical Decision Functions*; A. Ben-Tal, L. El Ghaoui & A. Nemirovski (2009), *Robust Optimization*; H. Rahimian & S. Mehrotra (2019), *DRO survey*.

#### `PR-20` — Sparsity & Compressed Sensing Worldview
- **Category:** Optimization, Inverse Problems & Control
- **Geometry:** `monopole-slider`
- **Poles:** `Belief in Underlying Low-Dimensional Sparsity`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** How deeply do you believe that high-dimensional natural signals, gene networks, and physical laws are inherently sparse or low-rank in the right basis?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Belief in Underlying Low-Dimensional Sparsity:* High-dimensional complexity is almost always supported on sparse bases or low-dimensional manifolds; $\ell_1$/nuclear-norm structure turns seemingly impossible underdetermined problems solvable.
- **Primary citations:** R. Tibshirani (1996), *JRSS-B* (LASSO); E. J. Candès, J. Romberg & T. Tao (2006), *IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory*; D. L. Donoho (2006).

---

### Category 5: Dynamical Systems, Stochastics & Complexity

#### `PR-21` — Deterministic Chaos vs. Stochastic Modeling
- **Category:** Dynamical Systems, Stochastics & Complexity
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Low-Dim Deterministic Attractors` | `Stochastic Perturbations on Dynamical Skeletons` | `Fundamentally Stochastic Processes`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** When real-world time series look jagged and unpredictable, do you model them primarily as deterministic nonlinear chaos or as stochastic differential equations and Markov processes?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Low-Dim Deterministic Attractors:* Strange attractors, bifurcations, and Takens delay embeddings reveal coherent low-dimensional nonlinear structure beneath apparent noise.
  - *Stochastic Perturbations on Dynamical Skeletons:* Combine deterministic drift/bifurcation skeletons with stochastic forcing to capture rare transitions, noise-induced tipping, and unresolved fast modes.
  - *Fundamentally Stochastic Processes:* In finance, ecology, and cellular copy numbers, intrinsic randomness and unresolved degrees of freedom make stochastic calculus (Itô/Stratonovich, jump processes) the natural primary language.
- **Primary citations:** E. N. Lorenz (1963); S. H. Strogatz (1994), *Nonlinear Dynamics and Chaos*; N. G. van Kampen (2007), *Stochastic Processes in Physics and Chemistry*.

#### `PR-22` — Gaussian Thin-Tail Convenience vs. Heavy-Tail Extreme Risk
- **Category:** Dynamical Systems, Stochastics & Complexity
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `CLT Gaussianity Is Sound Default` | `Robust Heavy-Tail Diagnostics` | `Extremistan Power-Law & EVT First`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Are Gaussian approximations robust workhorses for most applied modeling, or dangerous traps that blind analysts to power-law cascades and black-swan extremes?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *CLT Gaussianity Is Sound Default:* Finite-variance sums genuinely converge to Gaussians across countless physical and measurement domains; don't cry "power law" over every noisy tail.
  - *Robust Heavy-Tail Diagnostics:* Check tail indices carefully (Clauset et al.) and use Huber/Student-$t$ robustness so outliers never hijack inference without overclaiming universal power laws.
  - *Extremistan Power-Law & EVT First:* In finance, epidemics, networks, and disasters, a single tail event dominates the entire sum—Extreme Value Theory and fat-tail math are mandatory (Mandelbrot, Taleb).
- **Primary citations:** B. Mandelbrot (1963), *J. Bus.*; A. Clauset, C. R. Shalizi & M. E. J. Newman (2009), "Power-Law Distributions in Empirical Data," *SIAM Rev.*; P. Embrechts, C. Klüppelberg & T. Mikosch (1997), *Modelling Extremal Events*.

#### `PR-23` — Information Theory & MDL as Universal Organizing Principle
- **Category:** Dynamical Systems, Stochastics & Complexity
- **Geometry:** `monopole-slider`
- **Poles:** `Minimum Description Length & Entropy as Modeling North Star`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Should model selection, statistical inference, and machine learning all be grounded directly in Shannon information, KL divergence, and Minimum Description Length (compression)?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Minimum Description Length & Entropy as Modeling North Star:* Learning is compression; Shannon entropy, mutual information, and MDL unify hypothesis testing, regularization, and generalization under one timeless quantitative law.
- **Primary citations:** C. E. Shannon (1948), *Bell Syst. Tech. J.*; J. Rissanen (1978), *Automatica* (MDL); T. M. Cover & J. A. Thomas (2006), *Elements of Information Theory*.

#### `PR-24` — Mean-Field & Continuum Equations vs. Network & Agent Complexity
- **Category:** Dynamical Systems, Stochastics & Complexity
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Analytic Mean-Field & PDE Limits` | `Degree-Heterogeneous Network Theory` | `Detailed Heterogeneous Agent Models`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** In epidemiology, ecology, and socioeconomic dynamics, do clean compartmental mean-field ODEs (like SIR) capture the essentials, or do heterogeneous network hubs and individual agents invalidate them?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Analytic Mean-Field & PDE Limits:* Low-dimensional compartmental and hydrodynamic limits give crystal-clear epidemic thresholds ($R_0$), phase diagrams, and qualitative policy insight.
  - *Degree-Heterogeneous Network Theory:* Pair analytical rigor with network topology (scale-free hubs, community modularity, percolation) where connectivity variance fundamentally alters thresholds.
  - *Detailed Heterogeneous Agent Models:* Real human populations adapt behaviorally, cluster spatially, and break well-mixed assumptions—micro-level agent models capture crucial localized dynamics.
- **Primary citations:** W. O. Kermack & A. G. McKendrick (1927), *Proc. R. Soc. A*; R. Pastor-Satorras & A. Vespignani (2001), *Phys. Rev. Lett.*; M. E. J. Newman (2018), *Networks* (2nd ed.).

#### `PR-25` — Optimal Transport & Geometric Data Analysis
- **Category:** Dynamical Systems, Stochastics & Complexity
- **Geometry:** `linear-sliders`
- **Poles:** `Optimal Transport & Wasserstein Geometry` | `Topological Data Analysis (Persistent Homology)`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** How valuable are modern geometric-topological frameworks—lifting data into Wasserstein probability spaces or extracting persistent-homology shape summaries—compared to classical Euclidean statistics?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Optimal Transport & Wasserstein Geometry:* Wasserstein distances respect the underlying metric geometry of distributions cleanly where KL divergence blows up, unifying PDE gradient flows and generative modeling.
  - *Topological Data Analysis (Persistent Homology):* Multi-scale topological invariants uncover global loops, voids, and non-linear manifold structure completely invisible to linear PCA or pairwise correlations.
- **Primary citations:** C. Villani (2009), *Optimal Transport: Old and New*; G. Peyré & M. Cuturi (2019), *Found. Trends Mach. Learn.*; G. Carlsson (2009), "Topology and Data," *Bull. AMS*.

---

### Category 6: Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics

#### `PR-26` — Theorem-Proof Rigor vs. Pragmatic Working Heuristics
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Pragmatic Heuristics & Benchmarks First` | `Rigorous Core + Empirical Stress-Testing` | `Formal Theorem-Proof Guarantees Required`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** In applied math and statistics, should a new method be judged mainly by sharp theorems (consistency, minimax rates, convergence proofs) or by empirical performance on messy real benchmarks?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Pragmatic Heuristics & Benchmarks First:* Proving theorems often forces absurd assumptions (i.i.d. sub-Gaussian, Lipschitz constants known) that never hold in practice; test algorithms brutally on real problems.
  - *Rigorous Core + Empirical Stress-Testing:* Prove theoretical sanity on idealized baselines to understand failure modes, then verify robustness empirically on messy real datasets.
  - *Formal Theorem-Proof Guarantees Required:* Without minimax rates, stability bounds, and consistency proofs, applied math degrades into ad-hoc tinkering that collapses unpredictably on the next dataset.
- **Primary citations:** D. L. Donoho (2017), "50 Years of Data Science," *JCGS*; A. Gelman & C. R. Shalizi (2013); practitioner applied-math vs. ML tension.

#### `PR-27` — Domain Immersion vs. Generalist "Toolbox" Mobility
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Deep Single-Domain Immersion` | `Methodologist With Deep Domain Anchors` | `Agile Cross-Domain Mathematical Generalist`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** John Tukey quipped that the best thing about being a statistician is getting to play in everyone's backyard—should applied mathematicians specialize deeply in one science or roam freely across fields?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Deep Single-Domain Immersion:* Parachuting into a discipline without learning its measurement quirks, biology, or physics produces naive models that solve the wrong problem brilliantly.
  - *Methodologist With Deep Domain Anchors:* Build deep long-term partnerships in 1–2 empirical fields so real anomalies inspire general mathematical methods that transfer elsewhere.
  - *Agile Cross-Domain Mathematical Generalist:* Recognizing that the exact same PDE, stochastic process, or convex duality governs acoustics, finance, and neuroscience is applied mathematics' superpower.
- **Primary citations:** J. W. Tukey (1962), "The Future of Data Analysis," *Ann. Math. Stat.*; L. Breiman (2001).

#### `PR-28` — Reproducibility, Data Hygiene & Computational Workflows
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `monopole-slider`
- **Poles:** `End-to-End Scripted Reproducible Pipelines`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Should every statistical and modeling paper ship clean, end-to-end scripted pipelines (raw data cleaning through final tables/figures) with zero manual spreadsheet edits?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *End-to-End Scripted Reproducible Pipelines:* Data cleaning and preprocessing harbor far more consequential errors than exotic estimator choices; fully scripted, version-controlled pipelines are basic scientific hygiene.
- **Primary citations:** D. L. Donoho (2010), *Biostatistics*; R. D. Peng (2011), "Reproducible Research in Computational Science," *Science*; A. Gelman et al. (2020), *Bayesian Workflow*.

#### `PR-29` — Handling Model Misspecification ("All Models Are Wrong")
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `triangle`
- **Poles:** `Iterative Posterior Predictive Checking` | `Sandwich & Misspecification-Robust Inference` | `Model Averaging & Stacking Ensembles`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Since every statistical model is strictly misspecified relative to nature, how do you protect your conclusions?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Iterative Posterior Predictive Checking:* Continuously compare simulated data from your model against real observations—expanding and criticizing the model whenever discrepancies appear (Box's loop, Gelman).
  - *Sandwich & Misspecification-Robust Inference:* Use Huber–White sandwich variances, generalized estimating equations, and agnostic loss minimization so inference remains valid even when the model is wrong.
  - *Model Averaging & Stacking Ensembles:* Never wager on a single model; combine diverse model families via cross-validated stacking to hedge structural uncertainty directly (Yao et al.).
- **Primary citations:** G. E. P. Box (1980), *JRSS-A*; H. White (1982), *Econometrica*; Y. Yao et al. (2018), "Using Stacking to Average Bayesian Predictive Distributions," *Bayesian Anal.*

#### `PR-30` — Algorithmic Fairness, Accountability & "Weapons of Math Destruction"
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Purely Technical Accuracy Mandate` | `Transparent Fairness-Accuracy Trade-Offs` | `Ethical Impact & Refusal Duty First`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Because incompatibility theorems prove different mathematical fairness criteria cannot hold simultaneously when base rates differ, what is the applied mathematician's ethical duty in high-stakes models?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Purely Technical Accuracy Mandate:* Mathematicians should deliver the most calibrated, accurate statistical predictions possible and leave normative fairness choices to democratically accountable policymakers.
  - *Transparent Fairness-Accuracy Trade-Offs:* Audit disparate impacts, expose mathematical impossibility trade-offs explicitly to stakeholders, and quantify uncertainty around vulnerable subgroups.
  - *Ethical Impact & Refusal Duty First:* Models encode values and amplify historical bias under a veneer of mathematical objectivity; practitioners have a duty to contest or refuse harmful deployments (O'Neil).
- **Primary citations:** J. Kleinberg, S. Mullainathan & M. Raghavan (2017), *ITCS* (fairness impossibility); A. Chouldechova (2017), *Big Data*; C. O'Neil (2016), *Weapons of Math Destruction*.

#### `PR-31` — Communicating Deep Uncertainty to Decision-Makers
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Deliver Crisp Actionable Point Advice` | `Calibrated Probabilities + Plain Scenarios` | `Foreground Full Uncertainty & Ignorance`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** When advising policymakers or clinicians under severe uncertainty (pandemics, climate, financial crises), how much mathematical caveat helps versus paralyzes action?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Deliver Crisp Actionable Point Advice:* Overwhelming decision-makers with sprawling error bars invites paralysis or cherry-picking; synthesize your best calibrated recommendation clearly.
  - *Calibrated Probabilities + Plain Scenarios:* Communicate explicit probabilities (Brier-scored where possible) alongside plain-language best/expected/worst scenarios so risks are grasped intuitively (Spiegelhalter).
  - *Foreground Full Uncertainty & Ignorance:* Pretending precision you do not have destroys public trust when forecasts miss; candidly report structural uncertainty and where models stop knowing (Saltelli et al.).
- **Primary citations:** D. Spiegelhalter (2017), "Risk and Uncertainty Communication," *Annu. Rev. Stat. Appl.*; A. Saltelli et al. (2020), "Five Ways to Ensure That Models Serve Society," *Nature*.

#### `PR-32` — Working Style: Analytical Problem Formulation vs. Computational Data Craft
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `xy-switchbox`
- **Poles:** `X: Clean Mathematical Idealization ↔ Messy Raw Data Wrangling` | `Y: Pen-and-Paper Analysis ↔ Interactive Computational Prototyping`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Where do you feel sharpest and most in your element when attacking an applied modeling problem?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Clean Mathematical Idealization (X-Low):* Distilling a messy real-world system down to a canonical dimensionless equation or clean optimization program.
  - *Messy Raw Data Wrangling (X-High):* Getting your hands dirty with missingness patterns, censoring, measurement drift, and raw data quirks where real truth hides.
  - *Pen-and-Paper Analysis (Y-Low):* Deriving exact bounds, closed-form conjugate updates, stability criteria, and asymptotic behavior analytically.
  - *Interactive Computational Prototyping (Y-High):* Exploring simulations, MCMC diagnostics, cross-validation curves, and visual plots iteratively in code.
- **Primary citations:** J. W. Tukey (1962); D. L. Donoho (2017), "50 Years of Data Science," *JCGS*.

#### `PR-33` — Applied Mathematical Aesthetic Profile
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `star-radar`
- **Poles:** `Elegant Closed-Form Scaling` | `Sharp Minimax Optimality` | `Causal Identification Clarity` | `Decisive Out-of-Sample Calibration` | `Societal & Engineering Impact`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** What makes a piece of applied mathematics or statistics genuinely beautiful to you?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Elegant Closed-Form Scaling:* Collapsing a sprawling physical or statistical system onto one dimensionless master curve.
  - *Sharp Minimax Optimality:* Matching upper and lower information-theoretic bounds to prove no algorithm can possibly do better.
  - *Causal Identification Clarity:* A crystal-clear natural experiment or graphical identification proof that cleanly isolates cause from noise.
  - *Decisive Out-of-Sample Calibration:* Watching probabilistic forecasts hit real-world empirical frequencies right on the $45^\circ$ calibration line.
  - *Societal & Engineering Impact:* Seeing an algorithm, trial design, or PDE model directly save lives, prevent failure, or improve real decisions.
- **Primary citations:** Practitioner applied-mathematics and statistics aesthetic values; G. E. P. Box (1976); D. L. Donoho (2017).

#### `PR-34` — Open Benchmark Competitions vs. Real-World Deployment Validation
- **Category:** Applied Mathematical Practice, Rigor & Ethics
- **Geometry:** `trilinear-line`
- **Poles:** `Standardized Benchmarks Drive Progress` | `Rotating Blind & Real-World Audits` | `Live Deployment & Decision Outcomes Only`
- **Hovertip (`issue`):** Following David Donoho's "Common Task Framework," do leaderboards and public benchmarks accelerate applied math and ML, or breed Goodhart's Law overfitting to artificial test sets?
- **Per-pole hovertips:**
  - *Standardized Benchmarks Drive Progress:* Public datasets, fixed metrics, and blind test leaderboards (the Common Task Framework) are the secret engine behind modern predictive breakthroughs (Donoho).
  - *Rotating Blind & Real-World Audits:* Keep benchmarks honest by periodically refreshing private test sets, adding distribution-shift stress tests, and scoring calibration alongside accuracy.
  - *Live Deployment & Decision Outcomes Only:* Static leaderboards hide data leakage, feedback loops, and non-stationarity; only prospective performance in live deployment proves a model works.
- **Primary citations:** D. L. Donoho (2017), "50 Years of Data Science," *JCGS*; M. Hardt & B. Recht (2022), *Patterns, Predictions, and Actions*.

## 4. Canonical Archetypes (7 Archetypes)

### 1. The Classical Applied Mathematician & Asymptoticist
- **Summary:** Distills physical and natural systems into dimensionless conservation equations, boundary-layer asymptotics, scaling laws, and clean dynamical skeletons.
- **Primary Thinkers & Literature:** C. C. Lin & L. A. Segel (1974), C. M. Bender & S. A. Orszag (1978), G. I. Barenblatt (1996), S. H. Strogatz (1994).
- **Stance Profile:**
  - **Anchors High:** `PR-02` (*Strict Low-Dimensional Parsimony*), `PR-03` (*First-Principles Conservation & ODE/PDEs*), `PR-04` (*Back-of-the-Envelope & Matched Asymptotics*), `PR-05` (*Mechanistic Insight & Explanation*), `PR-14` (*Causal & Mechanistic Explanation*), `PR-21` (*Low-Dim Deterministic Attractors* / *Stochastic Perturbations on Dynamical Skeletons*), `PR-24` (*Analytic Mean-Field & PDE Limits*), `PR-32` (*Clean Mathematical Idealization* + *Pen-and-Paper Analysis*), `PR-33` (*Elegant Closed-Form Scaling*).
  - **Anchors Low:** `PR-01` (*Algorithmic Black-Box Prediction First*), `PR-02` (*Embrace Overparameterized Double Descent*), `PR-03` (*Purely Empirical Statistical Learning*).

### 2. The Modern Bayesian Workflow Practitioner
- **Summary:** Builds rich hierarchical generative models with weakly informative priors, rigorous posterior predictive checking, stacking ensembles, and transparent uncertainty communication.
- **Primary Thinkers & Literature:** G. E. P. Box (1976, 1980), A. Gelman & C. R. Shalizi (2013), A. Gelman et al. (2020), A. M. Stuart (2010), D. Spiegelhalter (2017).
- **Stance Profile:**
  - **Anchors High:** `PR-01` (*Generative Stochastic Data Models* / *Hybrid Interpretable Predictive Craft*), `PR-06` (*Bayesian Coherent Updating* paired with frequentist calibration), `PR-07` (*Replace with Effect Sizes & Intervals* & *Switch to Bayes Factors & Posteriors*), `PR-08` (*Weakly Informative Regularizing Priors*), `PR-17` (*Full Bayesian Inverse Posterior*), `PR-28` (High *End-to-End Scripted Reproducible Pipelines*), `PR-29` (*Iterative Posterior Predictive Checking* & *Model Averaging & Stacking Ensembles*), `PR-31` (*Calibrated Probabilities + Plain Scenarios*).
  - **Anchors Low:** `PR-07` (*Retain p < 0.05 with Discipline*), `PR-01` (*Algorithmic Black-Box Prediction First*).

### 3. The Severe-Testing Frequentist & Minimax Theorist
- **Summary:** Grounds statistical science in objective error-rate guarantees, distribution-free finite-sample bounds, sharp minimax optimality, and strict confirmatory discipline.
- **Primary Thinkers & Literature:** J. Neyman & E. S. Pearson (1933), A. Wald (1950), D. G. Mayo (2018), M. J. Wainwright (2019), V. Vovk et al. (2005).
- **Stance Profile:**
  - **Anchors High:** `PR-06` (*Frequentist Error-Rate Control*), `PR-07` (*Tighten Threshold (p < 0.005)* or *Retain with Discipline*), `PR-09` (*Strict Finite-Sample Non-Asymptotic Bounds*), `PR-10` (*Distribution-Free & Conformal Guarantees* / *Semiparametric Targeted Efficiency*), `PR-15` (*Mandatory Preregistration & Multiverse Audits* / *Strict Split*), `PR-19` (*Strict Minimax Worst-Case Guarantees*), `PR-26` (*Formal Theorem-Proof Guarantees Required*), `PR-29` (*Sandwich & Misspecification-Robust Inference*), `PR-33` (*Sharp Minimax Optimality*).
  - **Anchors Low:** `PR-06` (*Bayesian Coherent Updating*), `PR-08` (*Elicited Subjective Domain Priors*), `PR-26` (*Pragmatic Heuristics & Benchmarks First*).

### 4. The Breimanite Predictive & Common-Task Empiricist
- **Summary:** Measures models ruthlessly by held-out predictive calibration, Common Task benchmarks, double-descent expressiveness, and modern ML workflows rather than parametric dogma.
- **Primary Thinkers & Literature:** L. Breiman (2001), D. L. Donoho (2017), M. Belkin et al. (2019), B. Efron (2020 predictive wing), M. Hardt & B. Recht (2022).
- **Stance Profile:**
  - **Anchors High:** `PR-01` (*Algorithmic Black-Box Prediction First*), `PR-02` (*Embrace Overparameterized Double Descent*), `PR-03` (*Purely Empirical Statistical Learning*), `PR-05` (*Accurate Out-of-Sample Forecast*), `PR-10` (*Distribution-Free & Conformal Guarantees*), `PR-14` (*Calibrated Predictive Accuracy*), `PR-16` (*Embrace Non-Convex Landscape & SGD*), `PR-26` (*Pragmatic Heuristics & Benchmarks First*), `PR-32` (*Messy Raw Data Wrangling* + *Interactive Computational Prototyping*), `PR-33` (*Decisive Out-of-Sample Calibration*), `PR-34` (*Standardized Benchmarks Drive Progress*).
  - **Anchors Low:** `PR-01` (*Generative Stochastic Data Models*), `PR-02` (*Strict Low-Dimensional Parsimony*), `PR-26` (*Formal Theorem-Proof Guarantees Required*).

### 5. The Causal-Inference Architect
- **Summary:** Refuses to confuse correlation with intervention—insisting on explicit potential outcomes, causal DAGs, clean natural experiments, and transparent identification assumptions.
- **Primary Thinkers & Literature:** D. B. Rubin (1974), J. Pearl (2000), J. D. Angrist & J.-S. Pischke (2010), G. W. Imbens (2020), M. A. Hernán & J. M. Robins (2020).
- **Stance Profile:**
  - **Anchors High:** `PR-10` (*Semiparametric Targeted Efficiency*), `PR-11` (*Potential Outcomes* [Rubin/Imbens wing] or *Structural Causal Graphs* [Pearl wing]), `PR-12` (*No Causation Without Manipulation* or *Quasi-Experimental Design-Based Pluralism*), `PR-13` (*Design-Based Reduced-Form Clarity* / *Sufficient-Statistics Bridge*), `PR-14` (*Causal & Mechanistic Explanation*), `PR-15` (*Strict Split: Exploratory vs. Confirmatory* / *Mandatory Preregistration*), `PR-33` (*Causal Identification Clarity*).
  - **Anchors Low:** `PR-01` (*Algorithmic Black-Box Prediction First* for policy decisions), `PR-14` (Low reliance on pure prediction without causal identification).

### 6. The Convex Optimization & Control Strategist
- **Summary:** Turns complex engineering, sensing, and decision problems into tractable convex programs, sparse recovery guarantees, and safety-certified robust control architectures.
- **Primary Thinkers & Literature:** S. Boyd & L. Vandenberghe (2004), R. E. Kalman (1960), A. Ben-Tal et al. (2009), E. J. Candès & T. Tao (2006), B. Recht (2019).
- **Stance Profile:**
  - **Anchors High:** `PR-05` (*Optimal Decision & Control*), `PR-16` (*Formulate Convex Problems Only* / *Convex Relaxations + Local Refinement*), `PR-17` (*Classical Variational Regularization*), `PR-18` (*Model-Based Optimal Control & Lyapunov Safety* / *Learning-Augmented MPC*), `PR-19` (*Distributionally Robust Optimization* / *Strict Minimax Worst-Case Guarantees*), `PR-20` (High *Belief in Underlying Low-Dimensional Sparsity*), `PR-25` (*Optimal Transport & Wasserstein Geometry*), `PR-33` (*Societal & Engineering Impact*).
  - **Anchors Low:** `PR-18` (*Model-Free Deep Reinforcement Learning*), `PR-19` (*Optimize Average Expected Performance* without robustness).

### 7. The Complexity, Fat-Tail & Societal-Risk Modeler
- **Summary:** Skeptical of thin-tailed equilibrium assumptions and false precision—focusing on heavy-tail extremes, heterogeneous networks, model humility, and ethical accountability in society.
- **Primary Thinkers & Literature:** B. Mandelbrot (1963), A. Clauset & C. R. Shalizi (2009), M. E. J. Newman (2018), A. Saltelli et al. (2020), C. O'Neil (2016), J. Kleinberg et al. (2017).
- **Stance Profile:**
  - **Anchors High:** `PR-05` (*Stress-Testing & Scenario Exploration*), `PR-21` (*Fundamentally Stochastic Processes* / *Stochastic Perturbations*), `PR-22` (*Extremistan Power-Law & EVT First* / *Robust Heavy-Tail Diagnostics*), `PR-24` (*Degree-Heterogeneous Network Theory* & *Detailed Heterogeneous Agent Models*), `PR-27` (*Deep Single-Domain Immersion*), `PR-30` (*Ethical Impact & Refusal Duty First* / *Transparent Fairness-Accuracy Trade-Offs*), `PR-31` (*Foreground Full Uncertainty & Ignorance*), `PR-34` (*Live Deployment & Decision Outcomes Only*).
  - **Anchors Low:** `PR-22` (*CLT Gaussianity Is Sound Default*), `PR-30` (*Purely Technical Accuracy Mandate*), `PR-31` (*Deliver Crisp Actionable Point Advice* hiding uncertainty).
